Figure C.5
:
Recursive structure of the exact tree-based optimization. At a non-terminal node, the algorithm enumerates candidate splits, recursively solves the left and right children for each split, sums the two continuation losses, and returns the split with minimum total loss together with the corresponding descendant assignments. At the final depth
L
, the algorithm still enumerates candidate splits, but then evaluates the two resulting leaves directly, assigning each leaf to either an archetype or the basin of ignorance. If candidate thresholds are restricted to observed split points, there are at most
O(d|S|)
candidate splits at node
S
, and evaluating them all costs
O(d|S|^{2})
locally.

Table C.1
:
Calibrated simulation design.

Component

Specification

Outcome

Index outcome, defined as the average of standardized consumption, food-security, and asset outcomes.

Calibration tree

Depth-two ignorance-aware tree estimated on individual-level IPW pseudo-outcomes, with
G=4
, minimum leaf size
20
, five candidate split points per variable, and constant cost
c_{\mathrm{cal}}=2.5
. Active covariates are Peru, baseline log consumption, and baseline asset index.

Stable effects

In predictive leaves,
\tau(x)
is set equal to the calibration-tree prediction. The larger non-Peru ignorance region is also pooled with the stable component.

Contaminated region

The small Peru ignorance region, approximately
4\%
of the sample. In this region,
\tau(x)\sim\mathrm{Cauchy}(0,s)
.

Heterogeneity parameter

Cauchy scale
s\in[0.1,3]
, which controls the amount of arbitrary heterogeneity.

Simulated data

Conditional on
\tau(x)
, outcomes are homoskedastic Gaussian with variance one and treatment is assigned by a Bernoulli design. The simulated IPW signal is unbiased for
\tau(x)
.

Estimators

In each replication, re-estimate the ignorance-aware tree using simulated
\hat{\tau}(x)
and
\hat{\eta}(x)^{2}
, for
c\in\{0.1,0.5,1,1.5,2\}
. Benchmarks are a no-abstention tree, CART, generalized random forests, and empirical Bayes with CART shrinkage.

Evaluation

Prediction error is computed on the stable prediction set. We also report the share of contaminated observations correctly assigned to ignorance and the total share assigned to ignorance.

Replications

One hundred Monte Carlo replications for each value of
s
.

Appendix D
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma D.1
.

For any
i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}
, let
X_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
be an arbitrary random variable and
\mathcal{F}
a class of uniformly bounded functions with envelope
\bar{F}
.
Let
\Omega_{i}|X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
be random variables independently but not necessarily identically distributed, where
\Omega_{i}
is a scalar. Let for some arbitrary
u>0,u^{\prime}\in(0,1]
,

\max\{\mathbb{E}[|\Omega_{i}|^{2-2u^{\prime}}|X],\mathbb{E}[|\Omega_{i}|^{2+u}|X]\}\leq B_{u,u^{\prime}},\quad\forall i\in\{1,\cdots,n\}.
In addition, assume that for any distribution
Q_{n}
on points
x_{1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
, for some
V_{n}\geq 0
, for all
n\geq 1
,

\int_{0}^{2\bar{F}}\sqrt{\log\Big(\mathcal{N}\Big(\eta,\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q_{n})\Big)\Big)}d\eta\leq\sqrt{V_{n}}.
Let
\sigma_{i}
be
i.i.d
Rademacher random variables independent of
(\Omega_{i})_{i=1}^{n},(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
.
Then for a constant
0<C_{\bar{F}}<\infty
that depends only on
\bar{F}
and
u
, for all
n\geq 1
, and for
\Omega_{i}\geq 0

\small\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})1\{\Omega_{i}>\omega\}\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]d\omega\leq\frac{C_{\bar{F}}}{u^{\prime}}\sqrt{\frac{B_{u,u^{\prime}}V_{n}}{n}}.

(D.1)

In addition, for
\Omega_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

\small\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\Omega_{i}\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]\leq\frac{C_{\bar{F}}}{u^{\prime}}\sqrt{\frac{B_{u,u^{\prime}}V_{n}}{n}}.

(D.2)

Proof of Lemma
D.1
.

We denote
X=(X_{1},\cdots,X_{n})

For Equation (
D.1
), versions of this lemma can be found in Lemma A.5 in
Kitagawa and Tetenov (2021)
and
Viviano (2024)
(Lemma D.4), whose complete proof is available on the additional supplementary material available online at
https://dviviano.github.io/projects/note_preliminary_lemmas.pdf
(Appendix E, proof of Lemma E.9). We introduce a small modification to the above two references.
Instead of defining
B
to be some upper bound on the second plus
u
moment of
\Omega_{i}
(e.g., greater than one), we define it using an exact equality, taking into account also the moment
\mathbb{E}[\Omega_{i}^{2-2u^{\prime}}|X]
and then divide by
u^{\prime}
. For example, for
u=1,u^{\prime}=1
, then
B
defines the maximum between the third moment of
\Omega_{i}|X
and one.
Following verbatim the proof of Lemma E.9 in
https://dviviano.github.io/projects/note_preliminary_lemmas.pdf
, we can write from the paragraph “Integral Bound"

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})1\{|\Omega_{i}|>\omega\}\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]d\omega

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{\int_{0}^{1}C_{\bar{F}}\sqrt{\frac{V_{n}}{n}}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}P(|\Omega_{i}|>\omega|X)}{n}}d\omega}_{(I)}

(D.3)

\displaystyle+\underbrace{\int_{1}^{\infty}C_{\bar{F}}\sqrt{\frac{V_{n}}{n}}\sqrt{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}P(|\Omega_{i}|>\omega|X)}{n}}d\omega}_{(II)}.

Here we bound
(II)
as in
Viviano (2024)
, and therefore write
(II)\leq C_{\bar{F}^{\prime}}\sqrt{V_{n}\max_{i}\mathbb{E}[|\Omega_{i}|^{2+u}|X]/n}\leq C_{\bar{F}^{\prime}}\sqrt{V_{n}B_{u,u^{\prime}}/n}
. For
(I)
, instead of bounding
P(|\Omega_{i}|>\omega|X)\leq 1
as in
Viviano (2024)
, we use
P(|\Omega_{i}|>\omega|X)\leq\mathbb{E}[|\Omega_{i}|^{2-2u^{\prime}}|X]/(\omega^{2-2u^{\prime}})
, which, after integrating out, give us

(I)\leq\frac{1}{u^{\prime}}C_{\bar{F}^{\prime}}\sqrt{V_{n}B/n}
. To prove the second claim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\Omega_{i}\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})|\Omega_{i}|\mathrm{sign}(\Omega_{i})\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}f(X_{i})|\Omega_{i}|\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

where
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=\mathrm{sign}(\Omega_{i})\sigma_{i}
. We can then write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}f(X_{i})|\Omega_{i}|\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}f(X_{i})\int 1\{|\Omega_{i}|\geq\omega\}d\omega\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\int\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}f(X_{i})1\{|\Omega_{i}|\geq\omega\}\Big|d\omega|X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{\sigma}_{i}f(X_{i})1\{|\Omega_{i}|\geq\omega\}\Big||X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}\Big]d\omega.

Finally, note that
\mathbb{P}(\tilde{\sigma}_{i}=1|\Omega,X)=\mathbb{P}(\sigma_{i}\mathrm{sign}(\Omega_{i})=1|\Omega,X)=1/2
which implies that
\tilde{\sigma}_{i}
are Rademacher random variables independent of
\Omega_{i},X
. We can then invoke Equation (
D.1
) to complete the proof.
∎

Lemma D.2
.

(
Vershynin (2018)
, Lemma 6.4.2)
Let
\sigma_{1},...,\sigma_{n}
be Rademacher sequence independent of
X_{1},...,X_{n}
. Suppose that
X_{1},\cdots,X_{n}
are independent. Then

\small\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(X_{i})-\mathbb{E}[f(X_{i})]\Big|\Big]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\Big|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma_{i}f(X_{i})\Big|\Big].

Lemma D.3

(
Devroye et al. (2013)
, Theorem 29.7)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
be classes of measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}
, and suppose that there exist finite constants
B_{\mathcal{F}},B_{\mathcal{G}}
such that

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F},\ x\in\mathcal{X}}|f(x)|\leq B_{\mathcal{F}},\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G},\ x\in\mathcal{X}}|g(x)|\leq B_{\mathcal{G}}.

Define the product class

\mathcal{F}\cdot\mathcal{G}:=\{x\mapsto f(x)g(x):f\in\mathcal{F},\ g\in\mathcal{G}\},

and for
n
points
x_{1},\cdots,x_{n}
, with distribution
Q_{n}
, the corresponding
\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,(F\cdot\mathcal{G}),L_{1}(Q_{n}))
covering number.
Then, for every
\varepsilon>0
,
Q_{n},n\geq 1

\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon,(\mathcal{F}\cdot\mathcal{G}),L_{1}(Q_{n}))\right)\leq\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2B_{\mathcal{G}}},\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q_{n})\right)\,\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2B_{\mathcal{F}}},\mathcal{G},L_{1}(Q_{n})\right).

Lemma D.4

(
Devroye et al. (2013)
, Theorem 29.6)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
k\geq 1
be classes of real
functions on
x
. Define
\mathcal{F}=\{f_{1}+\cdots+f_{k};f_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{j},j=1,···,k\}
. Then for every
\eta>0
,
x_{1}^{n}
with distribution
Q_{n}
, any
Q_{n}
,
n\geq 1,k\geq 1
,
\mathcal{N}(\eta,\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q_{n}))\leq\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathcal{N}(\eta/k,\mathcal{F}_{j},L_{1}(Q_{n}))

Lemma D.5

(
Viviano (2024)
, Lemma D.5)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{F}_{k}
be classes of bounded functions with VC dimension
v
and envelope
\bar{F}<\infty
. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}=\Big\{f_{1}(f_{2}+...+f_{k}),\hskip 9.24994ptf_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{j},\hskip 9.24994ptj=1,\cdots,k\Big\}.

For arbitrary points
x_{1}^{n}\in\mathcal{X}^{n}
with distribution
Q_{n}
, any
Q_{n}
, for any
n\geq 1,k\geq 2,v\geq 1
,

\int_{0}^{2\bar{F}}\sqrt{\log\Big(\mathcal{N}\Big(\eta,\mathcal{J},L_{1}(Q_{n})\Big)\Big)}d\eta<c_{\bar{F}}\sqrt{k\log(k+1)v}

for a constant
c_{\bar{F}}<\infty
that only depends on
\bar{F}
.

Lemma D.6

(
Devroye et al. (2013)
, Theorem 13.5(ii))
.

Let
g:\mathbb{R}^{d}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
be an arbitrary function and
consider the class of functions
\mathcal{G}=\{g+f,f\in\mathcal{F}\}
. Then the VC-dimension of
\mathcal{G}
equals the VC-dimension of
\mathcal{F}
.

Lemma D.7

(Moments of the centered square)
.

Fix
u\in[0,1/2]
. Let Assumption
3.1
hold.
Then there exists a universal constant
c<\infty
, such that for each
x\in\mathcal{X}

\max\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}]|^{2-2u}\big],\ \mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}]|^{3}\big]\Big\}\leq cB^{3}\,(M_{u}+M_{u}^{2}).

Proof.

Write
\varepsilon=\hat{\tau}(x)-\tau(x)
omitting
x
for brevity. Since
\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon]=0
and
\eta(x)^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]
,

\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}]=2\tau(x)\varepsilon+\bigl(\varepsilon^{2}-\eta(x)^{2}\bigr).

Using
|a-b|^{q}\leq 2^{q-1}(|a|^{q}+|b|^{q})
, for any
q\in\{2-2u,3\}
, and
|\tau(x)|\leq B
,

\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}]|^{q}\big]\leq 2^{q-1}(2B)^{q}\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{q}]+2^{q-1}\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon^{2}-\eta(x)^{2}|^{q}],

and

\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon^{2}-\eta(x)^{2}|^{q}]\leq 2^{q-1}\Big(\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{2q}]+\eta(x)^{2q}\Big).

Since
q\geq 1
, Jensen’s inequality gives

\eta(x)^{2q}=\big(\mathbb{E}[\varepsilon^{2}]\big)^{q}\leq\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{2q}],

so that

\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon^{2}-\eta(x)^{2}|^{q}]\leq 2^{q}\,\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{2q}].

Also, by Hölder’s inequality,

\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{q}]\leq\big(\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{2q}]\big)^{1/2}.

This implies that

\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{q}]\leq M_{u},\mathbb{E}[|\varepsilon|^{2q}]\leq M_{u}^{2}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}(x)^{2}]|^{q}\big]\leq 2^{q-1}(2B)^{q}M_{u}+2^{2q-1}M_{u}^{2}.

Since
q\leq 3
, both coefficients are bounded by
32(1+B^{3})
. Using the fact that
B\geq 1
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma D.8

(Covering number of a union)
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{F}_{m}
be classes of measurable functions on
\mathcal{X}
, and let

\mathcal{F}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{F}_{j}.

Then, for any probability measure
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
and any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q))\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{j},L_{1}(Q)).

Proof.

For each
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{j}
be an
\varepsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{F}_{j}
under
L_{1}(Q)
with cardinality
\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{j},L_{1}(Q))
. Then

\mathcal{C}:=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{C}_{j}

is an
\varepsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{F}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{F}_{j}
. Hence

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q))\leq|\mathcal{C}|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{m}\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{j},L_{1}(Q)),

which proves the claim.
∎

Lemma D.9

(Assouad’s lemma for the Hamming loss, Theorem 2.12 in
Tsybakov (2009)
)
.

Let
\{P_{\theta}:\theta\in\{-1,+1\}^{m}\}
be a family of probability measures, and for
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
let
\theta^{(j)}
denote the vector obtained from
\theta
by changing only its
j
-th coordinate. Suppose that

\mathrm{KL}\!\left(P_{\theta},P_{\theta^{(j)}}\right)\leq\kappa\text{ for all }\theta\in\{-1,+1\}^{m},\ j\in\{1,\dots,m\},

where KL denotes the KL-divergence.
Then, for every estimator
\widehat{\theta}=(\widehat{\theta}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\theta}_{m})
,

\sup_{\theta\in\{-1,+1\}^{m}}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\!\left[\sum_{j=1}^{m}1\{\widehat{\theta}_{j}\neq\theta_{j}\}\right]\geq\frac{m}{2}\Bigl(1-\sqrt{\kappa/2}\Bigr).

Proof.

The result follows directly from
Tsybakov (2009)
, Theorem 2.12.
∎

Lemma D.10

(Entropy bound for bounded linear classes)
.

Let
q:\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
satisfy

\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\|q(x)\|_{2}\leq U

for some
U\in(0,\infty)
, and consider the function class

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}}:=\bigl\{f_{\theta}(x)=q(x)^{\top}\theta:\ \|\theta\|_{2}\leq R\bigr\}

for some
R\in(0,\infty)
. Let
\mathcal{Q}
denote the collection of finitely supported probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
. Then
\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}},\,x\in\mathcal{X}}|f(x)|\leq RU
, and there exists a universal constant
c_{0}<\infty
such that

\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{1}(Q)\right)}\,d\varepsilon\leq c_{0}\,RU\,\sqrt{d}.

Proof.

The proof is standard
(
Devroye et al., 2013
)
.
For any
Q\in\mathcal{Q}
and any
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\|f_{\theta}-f_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{Q,1}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\bigl|q(x)^{\top}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\bigr|\,dQ(x)\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}}\|q(x)\|_{2}\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}\,dQ(x)\leq U\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}.

Hence an
(\varepsilon/U)
-cover of the Euclidean ball

B_{2}^{d}(R):=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R\}

induces an
\varepsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}}
under
L_{1}(Q)
, so

\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{1}(Q)\right)\leq\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon/U,B_{2}^{d}(R),\|\cdot\|_{2}\right).

By the standard covering bound for the Euclidean ball,

\mathcal{N}\!\left(\delta,B_{2}^{d}(R),\|\cdot\|_{2}\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{2R}{\delta}\right)^{d},

and therefore

\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{1}(Q)\right)\leq d\log\!\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right).

It follows that

\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}\!\left(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{lin}},L_{1}(Q)\right)}\,d\varepsilon\leq\sqrt{d}\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\!\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right)}\,d\varepsilon.

With the change of variables
t=\varepsilon/(2RU)
, the right-hand side becomes

2RU\sqrt{d}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log(1+t^{-1})}\,dt,

and the last integral is a finite universal constant. This proves the claim.
∎

D.1
Proofs of auxiliary lemmas for Theorem
3.3

Throughout, we will use the notation introduced in the proof of Theorem
3.3
, Appendix
B.3
.

D.1.1
Proof of Lemma
B.1

Since
N\geq 128(G-1)^{2}
and
G\geq 4
,
N/[128(G-1)]\geq G-1\geq 3
.
Thus
b\geq\max\{1,N/[256(G-1)]\}
. Since

Mb\leq\frac{N}{256},

a\geq\frac{N-Mb}{G-1}-1\geq\frac{255}{256}\frac{N}{G-1}-1.

Therefore it follows

a\geq N/[2(G-1)]
. Since
\underline{\kappa}\leq 1/[4(G-1)]
by assumption in Theorem
3.3
,

a\geq\underline{\kappa}N
. Moreover,

a\geq\frac{N}{2(G-1)}\geq 4(G-1).

Also,

a\geq\frac{N}{2(G-1)}=64\frac{N}{128(G-1)}\geq 32b.

Finally, since
b\geq 1
and
\underline{\eta}\leq K_{0}/32
,

\Delta=\frac{\underline{\eta}}{8\sqrt{b}}\leq\frac{\underline{\eta}}{8}\leq\frac{K_{0}}{8}.

Next, we prove the first inequality in (
B.9
). This inequality is equivalent to

16Mb\Delta\leq aK_{0}.

Since
\Delta=\underline{\eta}/(8\sqrt{b})
, it is enough to prove

2M\underline{\eta}\sqrt{b}\leq aK_{0}.

Using
2M\leq G-1
,
b\leq N/[128(G-1)]
, and

a\geq N/[2(G-1)]
, we have

2M\underline{\eta}\sqrt{b}\leq\underline{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{N(G-1)}{128}},aK_{0}\geq\frac{NK_{0}}{2(G-1)}.

Hence
2M\underline{\eta}\sqrt{b}\leq aK_{0}
follows if

\underline{\eta}\sqrt{\frac{N(G-1)}{128}}\leq\frac{NK_{0}}{2(G-1)}.

After squaring and simplifying, this condition is

N\geq\frac{1}{32}\frac{\underline{\eta}^{2}}{K_{0}^{2}}(G-1)^{3}.

This is implied by the sample-size condition in Theorem
3.3
.

Next, we prove the second inequality in (
B.9
). Note that because
0\leq\Delta\leq K_{0}/8
,
(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2}\leq K_{0}^{2}
; in addition, it follows immediately that
ab/(a+b)\leq b
for
a\geq 0
.
The last condition on
L
follows since
0\leq L=N-Mb-(G-1)\lfloor(N-Mb)/(G-1)\rfloor<G-1
.

D.1.2
Proof of Lemma
B.2

For fixed
k
,
u\mapsto\Phi_{k}(u)
is concave on
[0,k]
, since

u\mapsto au/(a+u)
is concave and the other terms are affine in
u
. Thus

\Phi_{k}(u)\geq\min\{\Phi_{k}(0),\Phi_{k}(k)\}.

At
u=0
,

\Phi_{k}(0)=kK_{0}^{2}-\left(\frac{ab}{a+b}-\frac{a(b-k)}{a+b-k}\right)(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2},

and

\frac{ab}{a+b}-\frac{a(b-k)}{a+b-k}=\frac{a^{2}k}{(a+b)(a+b-k)}\leq k.

Hence
\Phi_{k}(0)\geq k(2K_{0}\Delta-\Delta^{2})\geq kK_{0}\Delta
, using

\Delta\leq K_{0}/8
.
Next note that

k\mapsto\Phi_{k}(k)
, is concave on
[0,b]
, and
\Phi_{0}(0)=0
. Hence

\Phi_{k}(k)\geq\frac{k}{b}\Phi_{b}(b).

A direct calculation gives
\Phi_{b}(b)=4abK_{0}\Delta/(a+b)
. Since

a\geq 32b
by Lemma
B.1
,

\frac{4ab}{a+b}\geq 3b.

Thus
\Phi_{k}(k)\geq 3kK_{0}\Delta
. Combining the endpoint bounds proves the claim.

D.1.3
Proof of Lemma
B.3

Fix
h\in\{2,\dots,G\}
(therefore
h\neq 1
does not include the abstention group). We first show that

\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}

is either empty or a consecutive interval.

If
u^{\star}=1
, then by construction

\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}=\{x:\alpha(x)=h\}\setminus A.

The set
\{x:\alpha(x)=h\}
is a consecutive interval by assumption. Removing the
consecutive block
A
from it can produce a non-consecutive set only if

A\subseteq\{x:\alpha(x)=h\}.

But in that case every point in
A
has label
h
, so

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(A)=\{h\}.

This contradicts
u^{\star}=1
, since
h>1
. Hence

\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}
is either empty or consecutive.

Now suppose
u^{\star}=h^{\star}\in\{2,\dots,G\}
. If
h=h^{\star}
, then

\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h^{\star}\}=\{x:\alpha(x)=h^{\star}\}\cup A.

Since
h^{\star}\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(A)
, we have

\{x:\alpha(x)=h^{\star}\}\cap A\neq\varnothing.

Thus
\{x:\alpha(x)=h^{\star}\}
and
A
are two consecutive intervals with nonempty
intersection, so their union is consecutive.

If instead
h\neq h^{\star}
, then

\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}=\{x:\alpha(x)=h\}\setminus A.

As above, this set can fail to be consecutive only if

A\subseteq\{x:\alpha(x)=h\}.

But then every point in
A
has label
h
, so

\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(A)=\{h\},

which would force
u^{\star}=h
, contradicting
h\neq h^{\star}
. Therefore

\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}
is either empty or consecutive for every

h\in\{2,\dots,G\}
.

The sets
\{x:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}
,
h=2,\dots,G
, are pairwise disjoint by construction of

\widetilde{\alpha}
. Moreover, the number of nonempty sets among them
cannot increase. If
u^{\star}=1
, each set is obtained from the corresponding original
set by removing points in
A
. If
u^{\star}=h^{\star}\geq 2
, then the label
h^{\star}

was already used on
A
, because

\{x:\alpha(x)=h^{\star}\}\cap A\neq\varnothing,

and all other labels only lose points in
A
. Hence no new nonempty predictive label is
created. It remains to prove the loss comparison. For every
x\notin A
,
the contribution of
x
to
R_{\theta}(\alpha,\mu)
and to

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\mu})
is the same because
\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=\alpha(x)
and
\widetilde{\mu}=\mu
.

For every
x\in A
, we have
\tau_{\theta}(x)=(-1)^{g}K_{0}
. Also,

\alpha(x)\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(A)
. Therefore the original contribution of
x
under
\alpha,\mu
to the loss is

\sum_{h=2}^{G}(\mu_{h}-(-1)^{g}K_{0})^{2}\,1\{\alpha(x)=h\}+K_{0}^{2}\,1\{\alpha(x)=1\}=\lambda_{\alpha(x)}.

The new contribution of
x
under
\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\mu}
is

\sum_{h=2}^{G}(\widetilde{\mu}_{h}-(-1)^{g}K_{0})^{2}\,1\{\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=h\}+K_{0}^{2}\,1\{\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=1\}=\lambda_{u^{\star}}.

By definition of
u^{\star}
,

\lambda_{u^{\star}}=\min_{u\in\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}(A)}\lambda_{u}\leq\lambda_{\alpha(x)}.

Thus the pointwise contribution weakly decreases for every
x\in A
, and is unchanged
for every
x\notin A
. Summing over
x\in\mathcal{X}_{N}
gives

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\widetilde{\mu})\leq R_{\theta}(\alpha,\mu).

This proves the claim.

D.1.4
Proof of Lemma
B.4

Part 1: defect count and construction of selected anchors.

The goal of this part is to quantify how
\widetilde{\alpha}
fails to satisfy

\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
. We first need to introduce some notation.

For each
j\in\{1,\dots,J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\}
, define

t_{j}:=\left|\{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}:A_{g}\subseteq D_{j}\}\right|.

Let

I:=\left|\left\{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=1\text{ for all }x\in A_{g}\right\}\right|,

and define

J_{0}:=|\{j:t_{j}=0\}|,J_{\mathrm{anch}}:=|\{j:t_{j}\geq 1\}|.

Since every anchor block is either entirely ignored or entirely contained in exactly one
predictive interval, we have

G-1=I+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}t_{j}=I+J_{\mathrm{anch}}+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}(t_{j}-1)_{+}.

Define

\delta:=I+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}(t_{j}-1)_{+}.

Then

\delta=G-1-J_{\mathrm{anch}}.

Since
J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}=J_{\mathrm{anch}}+J_{0}\leq G-1
, it follows that

J_{0}\leq\delta.

Moreover, by the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
,

\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}\Longleftrightarrow\delta\geq 1.

Thus, under the maintained assumption
\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
,
we have

\delta\geq 1.

Define

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ign}}:=\left\{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}:\widetilde{\alpha}(x)=1\text{ for all }x\in A_{g}\right\}.

For each
j\in\{1,\dots,J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\}
such that
t_{j}\geq 2
, choose a subset

\mathcal{S}_{j}\subseteq\{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}:A_{g}\subseteq D_{j}\}

with

|\mathcal{S}_{j}|=t_{j}-1.

Such a choice is possible because

\left|\{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}:A_{g}\subseteq D_{j}\}\right|=t_{j}.

Now define

\mathcal{S}:=\{A_{g}:g\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{ign}}\}\cup\bigcup_{\{j:t_{j}\geq 2\}}\{A_{g}:g\in\mathcal{S}_{j}\}.

Thus
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\{A_{2},\dots,A_{G}\}
, every ignored anchor block is included in

\mathcal{S}
, and for every interval
D_{j}
with
t_{j}\geq 2
, exactly
t_{j}-1
of the
anchor blocks contained in
D_{j}
are included in
\mathcal{S}
. Hence

|\mathcal{S}|=I+\sum_{j=1}^{J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}}(t_{j}-1)_{+}=\delta.

Part 2: lower bound on the excess loss of each selected anchor.

The goal of this part is to prove the following inequality:

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in A_{g}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr)\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{8N},\qquad A_{g}\in\mathcal{S}.

(D.4)

Fix
A_{g}\in\mathcal{S}
. There are two cases. First suppose that
A_{g}
is ignored by
\widetilde{\alpha}
. Then

L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)=K_{0}^{2}
for every
x\in A_{g}
. By
(
B.11
),

L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\leq\frac{K_{0}^{2}}{1024},x\in A_{g}.

Since
|A_{g}|\geq a
, we get

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in A_{g}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr)\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{N}\left(1-\frac{1}{1024}\right)>\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{8N}.

Now suppose that
A_{g}\subseteq D_{j}
, where
D_{j}
contains at least two anchor blocks.
Write the anchor blocks contained in
D_{j}
as

A_{h},A_{h+1},\dots,A_{v},h<v.

Since
D_{j}
is a consecutive interval and each anchor block is either entirely ignored or entirely
contained in one predictive interval, the anchor indices contained in
D_{j}
are
consecutive. Therefore

t_{j}=v-h+1\geq 2.

By definition,

\nu_{j,\theta}^{\star}=\frac{1}{|D_{j}|}\sum_{z\in D_{j}}\tau_{\theta}(z).

On the anchor blocks contained in
D_{j}
, because the signs of
\tau_{\theta}(x)
alternate,

\left|\sum_{z\in D_{j}\cap\bigcup_{r=h}^{v}A_{r}}\tau_{\theta}(z)\right|\leq(a+L)K_{0}\leq(a+G-2)K_{0},

where the last inequality uses
L\leq G-2
from Lemma
B.1
.

Next, define

\mathcal{H}(D_{j}):=\{m\in\{1,\dots,M\}:H_{m}\cap D_{j}\neq\varnothing\}.

We claim that
|\mathcal{H}(D_{j})|\leq t_{j}
. To show this note that if
H_{m}\cap D_{j}\neq\varnothing
,
then, because
D_{j}
is a consecutive interval,
H_{m}
can only be immediately to the left of

A_{h}
, between two anchors contained in
D_{j}
, or immediately to the right of
A_{v}
.
Formally, one of the following three conditions holds:

2m+1=h,h\leq 2m<2m+1\leq v,2m=v.

Define

r:\mathcal{H}(D_{j})\to\{h,\dots,v\}

by

\displaystyle r(m):=h1\{2m+1=h\}+(2m+1)1\{h\leq 2m<v\}+v1\{2m=v\}

This map is injective, and hence

|\mathcal{H}(D_{j})|\leq|\{h,\dots,v\}|=v-h+1=t_{j}.

Since
|\tau_{\theta}(z)|=\Delta
for every
z\in H_{m}
, it follows that

\left|\sum_{z\in D_{j}\setminus\bigcup_{r=h}^{v}A_{r}}\tau_{\theta}(z)\right|\leq t_{j}b\Delta.

Also,

|D_{j}|\geq\left|\bigcup_{r=h}^{v}A_{r}\right|=\sum_{r=h}^{v}|A_{r}|\geq(v-h+1)a=t_{j}a.

Combining the preceding displays and using
t_{j}\geq 2
, we obtain

|\nu_{j,\theta}^{\star}|\leq\frac{(a+G-2)K_{0}+t_{j}b\Delta}{t_{j}a}\leq\frac{a+G-2}{2a}K_{0}+\frac{b\Delta}{a}.

Using Lemma
B.1
, it follows that

|\nu_{j,\theta}^{\star}|\leq\frac{161}{256}K_{0}.

Thus, for every
x\in A_{g}
, since
\tau_{\theta}(x)\in\{-K_{0},K_{0}\}
,

L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)=(\nu_{j,\theta}^{\star}-\tau_{\theta}(x))^{2}\geq\left(K_{0}-|\nu_{j,\theta}^{\star}|\right)^{2}\geq\left(\frac{95}{256}K_{0}\right)^{2}.

Combining this with (
B.11
) gives

L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\geq\left(\frac{95^{2}}{256^{2}}-\frac{1}{1024}\right)K_{0}^{2}>\frac{1}{8}K_{0}^{2}.

Since
|A_{g}|\geq a
, this proves (
D.4
).

Part 3: anchorless intervals and the set
\mathcal{M}_{0}
.

The goal of this part is to control the number of blocks
H_{m}
intersected by predictive
intervals
D_{j}
that contain no anchor block
A_{g}
.
Specifically, define

\mathcal{M}_{0}:=\left\{m\in\{1,\dots,M\}:\exists j\in\{1,\dots,J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\}\text{ such that }D_{j}\cap H_{m}\neq\varnothing\text{ and }t_{j}=0\right\}.

We first show that every predictive interval with
t_{j}=0
can intersect at most one
coded block
H_{m}
, and if it intersects
H_{m}
, then it is contained in
H_{m}
. Suppose

t_{j}=0
and
D_{j}\cap H_{m}\neq\varnothing
. If
D_{j}\not\subseteq H_{m}
, then, since

D_{j}
is consecutive and
H_{m}
lies immediately between
A_{2m}
and

A_{2m+1}
, the interval
D_{j}
must intersect either
A_{2m}
or
A_{2m+1}
. Let
this anchor be
A_{g}
, with
g\in\{2m,2m+1\}
. By
Lemma
B.3
,
A_{g}
is either entirely ignored or
entirely contained in one predictive interval. Since
D_{j}\cap A_{g}\neq\varnothing
, the
block
A_{g}
is not entirely ignored; hence it must be entirely contained in a predictive
interval. Because predictive intervals are disjoint and
D_{j}
intersects
A_{g}
, this
predictive interval must be
D_{j}
. Therefore
A_{g}\subseteq D_{j}
, contradicting

t_{j}=0
. Hence
D_{j}\subseteq H_{m}
.

For each
m\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, choose one index

j(m)\in\{1,\dots,J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\}
such that

D_{j(m)}\cap H_{m}\neq\varnothing,t_{j(m)}=0.

By the previous paragraph,

D_{j(m)}\subseteq H_{m}.

Since the blocks
H_{1},\dots,H_{M}
are pairwise disjoint, if
m\neq m^{\prime}
, then

D_{j(m)}\neq D_{j(m^{\prime})}
. Therefore
m\mapsto j(m)
is an injection from

\mathcal{M}_{0}
into the set
\{j:t_{j}=0\}
, whose cardinality is
J_{0}
. Thus

|\mathcal{M}_{0}|\leq J_{0}\leq\delta,

(D.5)

where the second inequality follows from Part 1 of this proof.

Part 4: nonnegative excess on clean coded blocks.

The goal of this part is to show that if
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
, then the local excess on

B_{m}:=A_{2m}\cup H_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
is nonnegative. For
m=1,\dots,M
, define

\mathcal{E}_{m}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in B_{m}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr)
.
We claim that

m\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq 0.

(D.6)

Fix
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
. First suppose that
A_{2m}
and
A_{2m+1}
lie in the
same predictive interval. Since that interval is consecutive, it contains all
x
in
B_{m}
.
Using (
B.12
)
28
28

28

Using

a(t-K_{0})^{2}+a(t+K_{0})^{2}=2aK_{0}^{2}+2at^{2},

we can reduce the three-term least-squares problem to the two-point identity
(
B.12
). Namely,

\frac{1}{N}\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{a(t-K_{0})^{2}+a(t+K_{0})^{2}+b(t-\theta_{m}\Delta)^{2}\right\}=\frac{1}{N}\left[2aK_{0}^{2}+\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{2a(t-0)^{2}+b(t-\theta_{m}\Delta)^{2}\right\}\right]=\frac{1}{N}\left(2aK_{0}^{2}+\frac{2ab}{2a+b}\Delta^{2}\right)\geq\frac{2aK_{0}^{2}}{N}.
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\inf_{t\in\mathbb{R}}\left\{a(t-K_{0})^{2}+a(t+K_{0})^{2}+b(t-\theta_{m}\Delta)^{2}\right\}=\frac{1}{N}\left(2aK_{0}^{2}+\frac{2ab}{2a+b}\Delta^{2}\right)\geq\frac{2aK_{0}^{2}}{N}.

Combining this with (
B.9
) and
a\geq 32b
(Lemma
B.1
) gives

\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq\frac{2aK_{0}^{2}}{N}-\frac{bK_{0}^{2}}{N}\geq 0.

Now suppose that
A_{2m}
and
A_{2m+1}
do not lie in the same predictive interval.
Since
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
, every predictive point of
H_{m}
belongs to a predictive
interval containing either
A_{2m}
or
A_{2m+1}
; all remaining points of
H_{m}
are
ignored. Let
\bar{s}_{m}^{\theta}=2m1\{\theta_{m}=-1\}+(2m+1)1\{\theta_{m}=1\}
. Define
k_{m}
as the number of points in

H_{m}
either ignored or assigned to a predictive interval containing

A_{\bar{s}_{m}^{\theta}}
, and define
u_{m}
as the number of points in
H_{m}
assigned to
a predictive interval containing
A_{\bar{s}_{m}^{\theta}}
. Then

0\leq u_{m}\leq k_{m}\leq b.

From Equation (
B.12
), it follows that

\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq\frac{1}{N}\Phi_{k_{m}}(u_{m}),

with
\Phi
denoted in Equation (
B.13
).
In addition, by Lemma
B.2
,

\Phi_{k_{m}}(u_{m})\geq 0.

Therefore
\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq 0
, proving (
D.6
).

Part 5: global summation.

The goal of this final part is to combine the bounds obtained above. Since
G
is even and
M=G/2-1
, the sets

B_{1},\ldots,B_{M},A_{G}

are pairwise disjoint and their union is
\mathcal{X}_{N}
, where

B_{m}:=A_{2m}\cup H_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}.

For each
m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}
, define

\mathcal{S}_{m}:=\mathcal{S}\cap\{A_{2m},A_{2m+1}\},\zeta_{m}:=|\mathcal{S}_{m}|,

and

\mathcal{M}_{1}:=\{m\in\{1,\ldots,M\}:\zeta_{m}\geq 1\}.

Also define

\mathcal{E}_{m}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in B_{m}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr),\qquad\mathcal{E}_{G}:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in A_{G}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr).

We first record two lower bounds that will be used below. First, for any
anchor block
A_{g}\subseteq\{A_{2},\ldots,A_{G-1}\}
that is not selected in

\mathcal{S}
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in A_{g}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr)\geq-\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{1024N}.

(D.7)

Indeed,
L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)\geq 0
for all
x
, while
(
B.11
) gives

L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\leq\frac{K_{0}^{2}}{1024},\qquad x\in A_{g}.

Since
|A_{g}|=a
for
g=2,\ldots,G-1
,
(
D.7
) follows.

Second, for every
bridge
block
H_{m}
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in H_{m}}\bigl(L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)-L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)\bigr)\geq-\frac{bK_{0}^{2}}{N}.

(D.8)

To see this, note again that
L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x)\geq 0
. Under the benchmark
partition
\alpha^{\theta}
, the block
H_{m}
is pooled with
A_{2m}
when

\theta_{m}=1
, and with
A_{2m+1}
when
\theta_{m}=-1
. The total benchmark
least-squares loss on the corresponding pooled anchor-and-bridge block is

\frac{ab}{a+b}(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2}.

Therefore, the benchmark contribution from
H_{m}
alone is no larger than this total
quantity. By (
B.9
),

\frac{1}{N}\frac{ab}{a+b}(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2}\leq\frac{bK_{0}^{2}}{N},

which proves (
D.8
).

We now lower-bound
\mathcal{E}_{m}
block by block. If
m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
, then

\zeta_{m}\in\{1,2\}
. For each selected anchor in
\mathcal{S}_{m}
,
(
D.4
) gives a contribution at least

aK_{0}^{2}/(8N)
. For each non-selected anchor in

\{A_{2m},A_{2m+1}\}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{m}
, we use
(
D.7
). Finally, for
H_{m}
, we use
(
D.8
). Hence, for every
m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
,

\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq\frac{\zeta_{m}aK_{0}^{2}}{8N}-\frac{(2-\zeta_{m})aK_{0}^{2}}{1024N}-\frac{bK_{0}^{2}}{N}.

(D.9)

If
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{1}
, then
\zeta_{m}=0
. When also
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{0}
,
(
D.6
) gives
\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq 0
. When instead

m\in\mathcal{M}_{0}
, we use the bounds
(
D.7
) for both anchors and
(
D.8
) for
H_{m}
. Thus, for every
m\notin\mathcal{M}_{1}
,

\mathcal{E}_{m}\geq-\left(\frac{2a}{1024}+b\right)\frac{K_{0}^{2}}{N}\,\mathbf{1}\{m\in\mathcal{M}_{0}\}.

(D.10)

For the final anchor block
A_{G}
, note that the benchmark assigns
A_{G}
to its own
group and
\tau_{\theta}(x)
is constant on
A_{G}
. Hence

L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x)=0
for all
x\in A_{G}
. Therefore
\mathcal{E}_{G}\geq 0
if

A_{G}\notin\mathcal{S}
, while (
D.4
) gives a lower bound
of
aK_{0}^{2}/(8N)
if
A_{G}\in\mathcal{S}
. Consequently,

\mathcal{E}_{G}\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{8N}\mathbf{1}\{A_{G}\in\mathcal{S}\}.

(D.11)

Combining (
B.15
),
(
D.9
), (
D.10
), and
(
D.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathcal{E}_{m}+\mathcal{E}_{G}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{8N}\left(\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\zeta_{m}+\mathbf{1}\{A_{G}\in\mathcal{S}\}\right)-\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{1024N}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}(2-\zeta_{m})-\frac{bK_{0}^{2}}{N}|\mathcal{M}_{1}|-\left(\frac{2a}{1024}+b\right)\frac{K_{0}^{2}}{N}|\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1}^{c}|.

By Part 1,

|\mathcal{S}|=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\zeta_{m}+\mathbf{1}\{A_{G}\in\mathcal{S}\}=\delta.

Moreover,

|\mathcal{M}_{1}|\leq\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}\zeta_{m}\leq\delta,|\mathcal{M}_{0}\cap\mathcal{M}_{1}^{c}|\leq|\mathcal{M}_{0}|\leq\delta

by (
D.5
). Also, since
\zeta_{m}\in\{1,2\}
for

m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}
,

\sum_{m\in\mathcal{M}_{1}}(2-\zeta_{m})\leq|\mathcal{M}_{1}|\leq\delta.

Therefore,

\displaystyle R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})

\displaystyle\geq\delta\left(\frac{a}{8}-\frac{3a}{1024}-2b\right)\frac{K_{0}^{2}}{N}.

Using
a\geq 32b
from Lemma
B.1
, we have

2b\leq\frac{a}{16},
which implies

\frac{a}{8}-\frac{3a}{1024}-2b\geq\frac{a}{32}.

It follows that

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\delta\,\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{32N}.

Finally, since
\delta\geq 1
from Part 1 under the maintained assumption

\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
, we conclude that

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{32N},

which proves (
B.16
).

D.1.5
Proof of Lemma
B.5

Since
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
, every anchor
A_{g}
is contained
in some
D_{j}
, and no
D_{j}
contains more than one anchor. Thus the
G-1
anchor
blocks
A_{2},\dots,A_{G}
lie in
G-1
distinct predictive intervals. Since

J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\leq G-1
,
J_{\widetilde{\alpha}}=G-1
, and every
D_{j}
contains
exactly one anchor.

Fix
m
. The intervals
D_{m}^{L}
and
D_{m}^{R}
are distinct because they contain distinct
anchors. We show
D_{m}^{L}\subseteq A_{2m}\cup H_{m}
. If
m=1
,
A_{2}
is the leftmost
block. If
m\geq 2
, a point of
D_{m}^{L}
strictly to the left of
A_{2m}
, together
with consecutiveness and
A_{2m}\subseteq D_{m}^{L}
, would force

D_{m}^{L}\cap A_{2m-1}\neq\varnothing
, contradicting that
D_{m}^{L}
contains exactly one
anchor. Similarly, a point of
D_{m}^{L}
strictly to the right of
H_{m}
would force

D_{m}^{L}\cap A_{2m+1}\neq\varnothing
. Hence
D_{m}^{L}\subseteq A_{2m}\cup H_{m}
. Therefore

L_{m}:=D_{m}^{L}\cap H_{m}
is an initial segment of
H_{m}
, and

D_{m}^{L}=A_{2m}\cup L_{m}
.

The argument for
D_{m}^{R}
is symmetric. Since
m\leq M
and
M=G/2-1
,

2m+2\leq G
. A point of
D_{m}^{R}
strictly to the left of
H_{m}
would force
intersection with
A_{2m}
, while a point strictly to the right of
A_{2m+1}
would
force intersection with
A_{2m+2}
. Both contradict the one-anchor property. Hence

D_{m}^{R}\subseteq H_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
, so
R_{m}:=D_{m}^{R}\cap H_{m}
is a terminal segment of

H_{m}
, and
D_{m}^{R}=R_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
.

No third predictive interval can meet
B_{m}:=A_{2m}\cup H_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
. Indeed, if

D_{j}\cap B_{m}\neq\varnothing
and
D_{j}\neq D_{m}^{L},D_{m}^{R}
, then

D_{j}\cap A_{2m}=D_{j}\cap A_{2m+1}=\varnothing
, so
D_{j}\cap B_{m}\subseteq H_{m}
. If

D_{j}
contained any anchor to the left of
A_{2m}
, consecutiveness would force
intersection with
A_{2m}
; if it contained any anchor to the right of
A_{2m+1}
,
consecutiveness would force intersection with
A_{2m+1}
. Thus
D_{j}
would contain no
anchor, contradicting the first paragraph.

Therefore the only predictive intervals meeting
B_{m}
are
D_{m}^{L}
and
D_{m}^{R}
.
Since they are disjoint,
L_{m}\cap R_{m}=\varnothing
, and every point of

H_{m}\setminus(L_{m}\cup R_{m})
is ignored. The representation of
L_{m}
and
R_{m}
as an
initial and terminal segment of
H_{m}=\{x_{q_{m}},\dots,x_{q_{m}+b-1}\}
, and the identity

l_{m}+r_{m}+s_{m}=b
, follow directly from the definitions.

D.1.6
Proof of Lemma
B.6

For
m=1,\dots,M
, set
B_{m}:=A_{2m}\cup H_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
and denote

\ell_{m}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star}):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in B_{m}}L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}(x),\ell_{m}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta}):=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\in B_{m}}L_{\mathrm{bench}}(x).

Define

u_{m}:=r_{m}1\{\theta_{m}=1\}+l_{m}1\{\theta_{m}=-1\}

Then
0\leq u_{m}\leq k_{m}\leq b
,
k_{m}-u_{m}=s_{m}
. Also, by construction,

b-k_{m}=l_{m}1\{\theta_{m}=1\}+r_{m}1\{\theta_{m}=-1\}
.
Using Lemma
B.5
and (
B.12
), we can write

\ell_{m}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})=\frac{1}{N}\left[\frac{a(b-k_{m})}{a+b-k_{m}}(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2}+\frac{au_{m}}{a+u_{m}}(K_{0}+\Delta)^{2}+(k_{m}-u_{m})K_{0}^{2}\right].

(D.12)

Equation (
D.12
) follows from the fact that if
\theta_{m}=+1
, the first term corresponds to
A_{2m}\cup L_{m}
and the
second to
R_{m}\cup A_{2m+1}
; if
\theta_{m}=-1
, the roles of
l_{m}
and
r_{m}
are
reversed. The ignored portion has size
s_{m}=k_{m}-u_{m}
.

Under the benchmark rule, all
x\in H_{m}
have label

2m
if
\theta_{m}=+1
, and
2m+1
if
\theta_{m}=-1
. The
other adjacent anchor to
H_{m}
which is not pooled with
H_{m}
(
A_{2m}
if
\theta_{m}=-1
and
A_{2m+1}
if
\theta_{m}=1)
contributes zero loss by construction. Hence

\ell_{m}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})=\frac{1}{N}\frac{ab}{a+b}(K_{0}-\Delta)^{2}.

(D.13)

Subtracting (
D.13
) from (
D.12
) gives

\ell_{m}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-\ell_{m}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})=\frac{1}{N}\Phi_{k_{m}}(u_{m}).

By Lemma
B.2
,

\ell_{m}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-\ell_{m}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\frac{K_{0}\Delta}{N}k_{m}.

Summing over
m
, and using that
B_{1},\dots,B_{M},A_{G}
form a disjoint partition of

\mathcal{X}_{N}
, the contribution on
A_{G}
is nonnegative because the benchmark loss on

A_{G}
is zero and
L_{\widetilde{\alpha}}\geq 0
. This proves (
B.17
).

D.1.7
Proof of Lemma
B.7

If
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
, then Lemma
B.5

gives
q_{m}(\widetilde{\alpha})=|L_{m}|=l_{m}
. If
\theta_{m}=+1
and

\widehat{\theta}_{m}\neq\theta_{m}
, then
l_{m}<b/2
, so

k_{m}=r_{m}+s_{m}=b-l_{m}>b/2
. If
\theta_{m}=-1
and

\widehat{\theta}_{m}\neq\theta_{m}
, then
l_{m}\geq b/2
, so

k_{m}=l_{m}+s_{m}\geq b/2
. Hence, for
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
,

k_{m}\geq\frac{b}{2}\,\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\theta}_{m}\neq\theta_{m}\}.

Summing and applying Lemma
B.6
,

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\frac{bK_{0}\Delta}{2N}d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)
if
\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
.
If
\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}
, Lemma
B.4

gives

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{32N}.

Combining the two expressions we have

\displaystyle R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{aK_{0}^{2}}{32N}\mathbf{1}\{\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}\}+\frac{bK_{0}\Delta}{2N}d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\mathbf{1}\{\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}\}.

Since
d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\leq M
,

d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)\mathbf{1}\{\widetilde{\alpha}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}\}\geq d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta)-M\mathbf{1}\{\widetilde{\alpha}\notin\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{good}}\}.

Using (
B.9
),
aK_{0}^{2}/(32N)-MbK_{0}\Delta/(2N)\geq 0
, so that

R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})-R_{\theta}(\alpha^{\theta},\mu^{\theta})\geq\frac{bK_{0}\Delta}{2N}d_{H}(\widehat{\theta},\theta).

Finally, (
B.14
) implies

R_{\theta}(\alpha,\mu)\geq R_{\theta}(\widetilde{\alpha},\nu_{\theta}^{\star})
, which proves
(
B.18
).

Appendix E
Additional extensions

E.1
Breakeven analysis on prediction error

In this section we present a complementary break-even analysis based on the prediction accuracy of the procedure. Let
\hat{R}^{(m)},\hat{\pi}^{(m)},\hat{f}^{(m)}
be as defined in Section
4.1.1
.
We will adopt the convention that
R^{(0)}(f)
denotes
R(f,\pi=1)
where all units are in the prediction set and
f
is the prediction function and
\widehat{R}^{(0)}(f)
its in-sample estimator with
\hat{f}^{(0)}\in\mathrm{arg}\min_{f\in\mathcal{F}}\widehat{R}^{(0)}(f)
. Via sample splitting, take an independent validation sample as in
3.4
(Remark
1
), with
\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x)
denoting the estimated
\tau(x)
out-of-sample.
For each
m\in\mathcal{M}
, define

\Delta(m)=\sum_{x}p(x)\left[(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}-(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}\right]\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x),\widehat{\Delta}(m)=\sum_{x}p(x)\left[(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}-(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}\right]\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x)

as the difference in loss with respect to the population
\tau(x)
on the prediction set under the prediction rule
\hat{\pi}^{(m)}
and its estimator out-of-sample.
Negative values of
\Delta(m)
indicate that, under policy
\pi^{(m)}
corresponding to abstention cost
c_{m}
, the estimated ignorance-aware rule is better than the estimated prediction that predicts everywhere on the prediction set.

Denote, for any
m\in\mathcal{M}
(with
N_{\mathcal{X}}:=|\mathcal{X}|
)

\sigma_{m}^{2}=\mathbb{V}\left(\sqrt{N_{\mathcal{X}}}\widehat{\Delta}(m)|\mathcal{T}\right),\mathcal{T}=\left(\hat{f}^{(0)},\Big(\hat{f}^{(m)},\hat{\pi}^{(m)}\Big)_{m\in\mathcal{M}}\right)

the variance of
\sqrt{N_{\mathcal{X}}}(\widehat{R}_{oos}^{(m)}(\hat{f}^{(m)},\hat{\pi}^{(m)})-\widehat{R}_{oos}^{(0)}(\hat{f}^{(0)}))
, conditional on the training sample.
We take a variance estimate
S_{m}
, so that
P(S_{m}^{2}\geq\sigma_{m}^{2})\rightarrow 1
for any
m\in\mathcal{M}
. We provide formal expressions for
S_{m}^{2}
below. For any
j\leq|\mathcal{M}|
, we then construct a statistic

\displaystyle U_{j}=\max_{l\geq j}\left\{\widehat{\Delta}(m_{l})+\Phi^{-1}(1-\alpha)\cdot\frac{S_{m_{l}}}{\sqrt{N_{\mathcal{X}}}}\right\}

(E.1)

where
\Phi(\cdot)
is the CDF of a Standard Normal random variable, and
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. As in Section
4.1.1
, we choose

\hat{j}^{\star}=\min\{j:U_{j}<0\},\hat{m}^{\star}=m_{\hat{j}^{\star}}

with the convention that if
U_{j}\geq 0
for all
j
,
\hat{m}^{\star}=\infty
.

Theorem E.1

(Inference for break-even analysis)
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.3
hold for the in-sample and out-of-sample estimates and assume the two are independent. Let
\sigma_{m}^{2}>\underline{l}
for a positive constant
\underline{l}>0
almost surely for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
, with
|\mathcal{M}|
finite. Take any
S_{m}^{2}
so that
\lim_{N_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\infty}P(S_{m}^{2}\geq\sigma_{m}^{2})=1
for all
m\in\mathcal{M}
. Then

\limsup_{N_{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\infty}P(\hat{m}^{\star}<\infty,\Delta(\hat{m}^{\star})\geq 0)\leq\alpha.

See Appendix
F.7
for the proof. We are left to derive an expression for a uniform bound
S_{m}^{2}
. Take
\xi_{x}^{(m)}=\Big\{(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}-(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}\Big\}\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x)
.
A natural approach is to take
S_{m}^{2}=N_{\mathcal{X}}\sum_{x}p(x)^{2}\mathbb{E}[(\xi_{x}^{(m)}-\mathbb{E}[\bar{\xi}^{m}|\mathcal{T}])^{2}|\mathcal{T}]
where
\bar{\xi}^{m}=\sum_{x}p(x)\xi_{x}^{(m)}
.

By replacing the expectations with the empirical average, this approach would provide us with a consistent estimate of a valid upper bound
S_{m}^{2}
(which averages across types), where
\mathbb{E}[\bar{\xi}^{m}|\mathcal{T}]
can be consistently estimated by the law of large numbers (since
\xi_{x}
are independent conditional on
\mathcal{T}
and have bounded second moment).

E.2
Approximate clustering with gradient descent

Algorithm
E.1
describes in details gradient descent for clustering methods. In practice, because the algorithm may not converge to optimum we recommend comparing its solution against the exact solution with hard stopping time to compare the two.

Algorithm E.1

Approximate multi-dimensional clustering with abstention

1:

Types
x_{1},\dots,x_{|\mathcal{X}|}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, estimates
\hat{\tau}(x_{i})
, variance estimates
\hat{\eta}(x_{i})^{2}
, abstention costs
\hat{c}(x_{i})
, weights
p(x_{i})
, number of predictive groups
G-1
, temperature
\rho>0
, smooth-min parameter
\lambda>0
, initial centroids
z^{(0)}=(z_{2}^{(0)},\dots,z_{G}^{(0)})
, minimum cluster size
\underline{\kappa}|\mathcal{X}|
, step sizes
(\gamma_{t})_{t\geq 0}

2:

for

t=0,1,2,\dots

do

3:

Soft cluster weights:
for each
i
and
g\in\{2,\dots,G\}
, set

w_{ig}^{(t)}:=\frac{\exp\!\left(-\|x_{i}-z_{g}^{(t)}\|^{2}/\rho\right)}{\sum_{h=2}^{G}\exp\!\left(-\|x_{i}-z_{h}^{(t)}\|^{2}/\rho\right)}.

4:

Profiled weighted cluster means:
for each
g\in\{2,\dots,G\}
, define, for any candidate centroids
z
,

\hat{\mu}_{g}(z):=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}p(x_{i})\,w_{ig}(z,\rho)\,\hat{\tau}(x_{i})}{\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}p(x_{i})\,w_{ig}(z,\rho)}.

5:

Profiled soft prediction and ignorance costs:
for each
g\in\{2,\dots,G\}
, define

\widehat{C}_{g,\rho}^{\mathrm{pred}}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}p(x_{i})\,w_{ig}(z,\rho)\Big\{\big(\hat{\mu}_{g}(z)-\hat{\tau}(x_{i})\big)^{2}-\hat{\eta}(x_{i})^{2}\Big\},

and

\widehat{C}_{g,\rho}^{\mathrm{ign}}(z):=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}p(x_{i})\,w_{ig}(z,\rho)\,\hat{c}(x_{i}).

6:

Smoothed profiled empirical loss:
define

\widehat{Q}_{\rho,\lambda}(z):=\sum_{g=2}^{G}\operatorname{smin}_{\lambda}\left\{\widehat{C}_{g,\rho}^{\mathrm{pred}}(z),\widehat{C}_{g,\rho}^{\mathrm{ign}}(z)\right\},

where

\operatorname{smin}_{\lambda}(a,b):=-\lambda\log\left(\exp(-a/\lambda)+\exp(-b/\lambda)\right).

As
\lambda\downarrow 0
,
\operatorname{smin}_{\lambda}(a,b)
approaches
\min\{a,b\}
.

7:

Gradient step:
update the centroids by

z^{(t+1)}=z^{(t)}-\gamma_{t}\nabla_{z}\widehat{Q}_{\rho,\lambda}(z^{(t)}).

8:

Hard assignment:
for each
i
, set

\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i}):=\arg\min_{g\in\{2,\dots,G\}}\|x_{i}-z_{g}^{(t+1)}\|^{2}.

9:

for
each cluster
g\in\{2,\dots,G\}

do

10:

Define the profiled hard-cluster mean

\hat{\mu}_{g}^{(t+1)}:=\frac{\sum_{i:\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g}p(x_{i})\hat{\tau}(x_{i})}{\sum_{i:\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g}p(x_{i})}.

11:

Define the empirical loss from keeping cluster
g
predictive and from assigning it to ignorance as

\widehat{L}_{g}^{\mathrm{pred}}:=\sum_{i:\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g}p(x_{i})\Big\{\big(\hat{\mu}_{g}^{(t+1)}-\hat{\tau}(x_{i})\big)^{2}-\hat{\eta}(x_{i})^{2}\Big\},\hskip 17.00024pt\widehat{L}_{g}^{\mathrm{ign}}:=\sum_{i:\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g}p(x_{i})\hat{c}(x_{i}).

12:

if

\#\{i:\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g\}<\underline{\kappa}|\mathcal{X}|
or
\widehat{L}_{g}^{\mathrm{pred}}>\widehat{L}_{g}^{\mathrm{ign}}

then

13:

Set
\pi^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=0
for all
i
with
\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g

14:

else

15:

Set
\pi^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=1
for all
i
with
\alpha^{(t+1)}(x_{i})=g

16:

end

if

17:

end

for

18:

if

z^{(t+1)}
and
\pi^{(t+1)}
have converged
then

19:

break

20:

end

if

21:

end

for

22:

return
final centroids
z^{(t+1)}
, partition
\alpha^{(t+1)}
, and abstention rule
\pi^{(t+1)}

Appendix F
Proofs for extensions

F.1
Proof of Lemma
A.1

For each
x\in\mathcal{X}
, define

\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi):=\left\{\bigl(f(x)-\hat{\tau}(x)\bigr)^{2}-\hat{\eta}(x)^{2}\right\}\pi(x)+\hat{c}(x)\bigl(1-\pi(x)\bigr).

By the law of iterated expectations,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{x}\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{x}\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\mid S_{x}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{x}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\mid S_{x}\right]\right].

Next, using Assumption
A.1
(ii),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[\bigl(f(x)-\hat{\tau}(x)\bigr)^{2}-\hat{\eta}(x)^{2}\mid S_{x}\right]=\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}+\mathbb{V}(\hat{\tau}(x)\mid S_{x})-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\eta}(x)^{2}\mid S_{x}]=\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2},

and

\mathbb{E}[\hat{c}(x)\mid S_{x}]=c(x).

Therefore

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\mid S_{x}\right]=\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\pi(x)+c(x)\bigl(1-\pi(x)\bigr).

Substituting back,

\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{x}\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\right]=\mathbb{E}[S_{x}]\,\Big(\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\pi(x)+c(x)\bigl(1-\pi(x)\bigr)\Big).

By Assumption
A.1
(i),
\mathbb{E}[S_{x}]=N_{S}p(x)
, so

\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{x}\widehat{\rho}_{x}(f,\pi)\right]=N_{S}p(x)\,\Big(\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\pi(x)+c(x)\bigl(1-\pi(x)\bigr)\Big).

Summing over
x\in\mathcal{X}
and dividing by
N_{S}
yields

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{R}_{S}(f,\pi)]=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\Big(\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\pi(x)+c(x)\bigl(1-\pi(x)\bigr)\Big)=R_{c}(f,\pi).

F.2
Proof of Theorem
A.2

First, we introduce some notation. We denote

\tau_{i}:=\tau(x_{i}),\hat{\tau}_{i}:=\hat{\tau}(x_{i}),\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}:=\hat{\eta}(x_{i})^{2},\hat{c}_{i}:=\hat{c}(x_{i}),c_{i}:=c(x_{i}),S_{i}:=S_{x_{i}},

and let

\lambda_{i}:=\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}.

By Assumption
A.1
(i),

0\leq\lambda_{i}\leq 1\text{ almost surely,}

and

\mathbb{E}[n_{S}]=\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\mathbb{E}[S_{i}]=N_{S}.

Define

\widehat{R}_{S}(f,\pi):=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}S_{i}\left[\left\{\bigl(f(x_{i})-\hat{\tau}_{i}\bigr)^{2}-\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\right\}\pi(x_{i})+\hat{c}_{i}\bigl(1-\pi(x_{i})\bigr)\right].

Let

(\pi^{\star},f^{\star})\in\arg\min_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}R_{c}(f,\pi).

Since
(\hat{f}_{S},\hat{\pi}_{S})
minimizes
\widehat{R}_{S}(f,\pi)
over
(\mathcal{F},\Pi)
,

\widehat{R}_{S}(\hat{f}_{S},\hat{\pi}_{S})\leq\widehat{R}_{S}(f^{\star},\pi^{\star}).

Therefore,

R_{c}(\hat{f}_{S},\hat{\pi}_{S})-R_{c}(f^{\star},\pi^{\star})\leq\bigl(R_{c}-\widehat{R}_{S}\bigr)(\hat{f}_{S},\hat{\pi}_{S})+\bigl(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c}\bigr)(f^{\star},\pi^{\star})\leq 2\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|.

(F.1)

Hence it suffices to bound

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right].

Step 1: Symmetrization of the centered empirical process.

For every
(f,\pi)
, define

\mathcal{Z}_{i}(f,\pi):=S_{i}\left[\left\{\bigl(f(x_{i})-\hat{\tau}_{i}\bigr)^{2}-\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\right\}\pi(x_{i})+\hat{c}_{i}\bigl(1-\pi(x_{i})\bigr)\right].

Then by Lemma
A.1
,

\mathbb{E}[\widehat{R}_{S}(f,\pi)]=R_{c}(f,\pi)
, which implies we can write

(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)=\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\Big(\mathcal{Z}_{i}(f,\pi)-\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{i}(f,\pi)]\Big).

Let
\sigma_{i}
be i.i.d. Rademacher random variables independent of the data. By Lemma
D.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right]\leq 2\,\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\frac{1}{N_{S}}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{X}|}\sigma_{i}\mathcal{Z}_{i}(f,\pi)\right|\right].

Using

\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]=\tau_{i}^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}],

which follows from Assumption
A.1
(ii), we can rewrite (adding and subtracting the relevant terms)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{i}(f,\pi)=S_{i}\Big[\bigl(\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]\bigr)\pi(x_{i})-2f(x_{i})(\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i})\pi(x_{i})-\bigl(\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]\bigr)\pi(x_{i})

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt\hskip 18.49988pt+(\hat{c}_{i}-c_{i})\bigl(1-\pi(x_{i})\bigr)+\bigl(f(x_{i})-\tau_{i}\bigr)^{2}\pi(x_{i})+c_{i}\bigl(1-\pi(x_{i})\bigr)\Big].

Therefore, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right]\leq\frac{2\bar{p}}{N_{S}}\mathbb{E}[T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}+T_{5}+T_{6}],

(F.2)

where we have

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{\sup}_{\pi\in\Pi}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}\bigl(\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]\bigr)\pi(x_{i})\right|,\hskip 8.50012ptT_{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 2K\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}\frac{f(x_{i})}{K}(\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i})\pi(x_{i})\right|

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathbb{\sup}_{\pi\in\Pi}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}\bigl(\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]\bigr)\pi(x_{i})\right|,T_{4}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}(\hat{c}_{i}-c_{i})(1-\pi(x_{i}))\right|,

\displaystyle T_{5}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}(K+B)^{2}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}\frac{\bigl(f(x_{i})-\tau_{i}\bigr)^{2}}{(K+B)^{2}}\pi(x_{i})\right|,T_{6}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sup_{\pi\in\Pi}\left|\sum_{i}\sigma_{i}\frac{S_{i}}{\bar{p}}c_{i}(1-\pi(x_{i}))\right|,

where for
T_{2}
and
T_{5}
we divided and multiply by the upper envelope
K
and
(K+B)^{2}
respectively.
Next, we bound each of these expectations conditional on the vector
(S)_{i=1}^{n}
and then apply the law of iterated expectations.

Step 2: complexity bounds.

The following complexity bounds hold.

(i)

By the same argument as in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, Assumption
3.2
implies that, for any probability measure
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
,

\int_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\Pi,L_{1}(Q))}\,d\varepsilon\leq c_{0}\sqrt{v_{\Pi}}

for a universal constant
c_{0}<\infty
. The same bound also holds for the class
\{1-\pi:\pi\in\Pi\}
by Lemma
D.6
.

(ii)

Similarly, following Step 4 of the proof of Theorem
3.1
, We can bound the covering number of the function class
\mathcal{H};=\{x\mapsto\frac{f(x)}{K}\pi(x):f\in\mathcal{F},\pi\in\Pi\}
, so that
\int_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\log(\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{H},L_{1}(Q))}d\varepsilon\leq c_{0}\sqrt{C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi}}
for a universal constant
c_{0}<\infty
.

(iii)

Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{1}:=\left\{x\mapsto\frac{\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}}{(K+B)^{2}}\pi(x):f\in\mathcal{F},\ \pi\in\Pi\right\},\hskip 18.49988pt\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{2}:=\left\{x\mapsto\frac{\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}}{(K+B)^{2}}:f\in\mathcal{F}\right\}.

Since
|f(x)|\leq K
and
|\tau(x)|\leq B
,

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{2},\ x\in\mathcal{X}}|g(x)|\leq 1.

Moreover, for any
f,g\in\mathcal{F}
,

\bigl|(f(x)-\tau(x))^{2}-(g(x)-\tau(x))^{2}\bigr|=|f(x)-g(x)|\,|f(x)+g(x)-2\tau(x)|\leq 2(K+B)|f(x)-g(x)|.

Therefore, since
\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{2}
divides by
(K+B)^{2}

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{2},L_{1}(Q))\leq\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon(K+B)}{4},\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q)\right).

By Lemma
D.3
,

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{G}_{1},L_{1}(Q))\leq\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\mathcal{F}_{\tau}^{2},L_{1}(Q)\right)\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\Pi,L_{1}(Q)\right)\leq\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon(K+B)}{4},\mathcal{F},L_{1}(Q)\right)\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\Pi,L_{1}(Q)\right).

Hence, from a simple change of variable

\sup_{Q}\int_{0}^{2}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{G}_{1},L_{1}(Q))}\,d\varepsilon\leq c_{0}^{\prime}\Big(\frac{1}{(K+B)}\sqrt{C(\mathcal{F})}+\sqrt{v_{\Pi}}\Big)

for a universal constant
c_{0}^{\prime}<\infty
.

Step 3: Moments bound.

Using the complexity bounds in Step 2, we next invoke Lemma
D.1
for each term
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3},T_{4},T_{5},T_{6}
. To do so, it suffices to check that for each random variable in the summand the moment bound is sufficiently attained.
By Assumption
A.1
(iv), for all
i
,

\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}_{i}-\tau_{i}|^{3}\mid S_{i}\big],\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\tau}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]|^{3}\mid S_{i}\big],\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}-\mathbb{E}[\hat{\eta}_{i}^{2}\mid S_{i}]|^{3}\mid S_{i}\big],\mathbb{E}\big[|\hat{c}_{i}-c_{i}|^{3}\mid S_{i}\big]\leq M

and in addition
|c_{i}|^{3}\leq M
by assumption, and
|S_{i}|/\bar{p}\leq 1
almost surely.
Therefore, conditional on
S
, we can bound for any
j\in\{1,\cdots,6\}
using Lemma
D.1
, with
(K+B)_{+}^{2}=\max\{1,(K+B)^{2}\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[T_{j}|S]]\leq q_{0}(K+B)_{+}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\Big[\sqrt{M\sum_{i}1\{S_{i}>0\}(C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi})}|S\Big]\right]\leq q_{0}(K+B)_{+}^{2}\sqrt{MN_{\mathcal{X}}(C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi})}

for a universal constant
q_{0}
(where we use
\sum_{i}1\{S_{i}>0\}\leq N_{\mathcal{X}}
).

Step 4: law of iterated expectations

Using the expression in Equation (
F.2
), and collecting the terms,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,\ f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}_{S}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right]\leq\frac{2}{N_{S}}q_{0}(K+B)_{+}^{2}\bar{p}\sqrt{MN_{\mathcal{X}}(C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi})}.

Finally note that because
\mathbb{E}[S_{x}]>\gamma
,
N_{S}=\sum_{x}\mathbb{E}[S_{x}]\geq\gamma N_{\mathcal{X}}
. Therefore we have
\frac{2}{N_{S}}q_{0}(K+B)_{+}^{2}\bar{p}\sqrt{MN_{\mathcal{X}}(C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi})}\leq\frac{2}{\gamma N_{\mathcal{X}}}q_{0}(K+B)_{+}^{2}\bar{p}\sqrt{MN_{\mathcal{X}}(C(\mathcal{F})+v_{\Pi})}
which completes the claim after rearrangement.

F.3
Proof of Theorem
A.3

We verify Assumption
3.3
for
\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}}
and then invoke Theorem
3.1
. The argument is standard and follows from
Devroye et al. (2013)
;
Vershynin (2018)
among others.

Step 1:
l_{2}
-cover

Since
\|q(x)\|_{2}\leq U
and
\|\theta\|_{2}\leq R
, every
f_{\theta}\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}}
satisfies

|f_{\theta}(x)|\leq\|q(x)\|_{2}\|\theta\|_{2}\leq RU,\forall x\in\mathcal{X}.

Thus Assumption
3.3
holds with envelope
K=RU
, provided we bound the corresponding entropy integral. We now characterize
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}})
. This follows standard arguments in empirical process theory
(
Van Der Vaart et al., 1996
)
. Fix a probability measure
Q
on
\mathcal{X}
. For
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\Theta_{0}
,

\|f_{\theta}-f_{\theta^{\prime}}\|_{Q,1}=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\big|q(x)^{\prime}(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\big|\,dQ(x)\leq\int_{\mathcal{X}}\|q(x)\|_{2}\,\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}\,dQ(x)\leq U\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|_{2}.

(F.3)

Therefore, an
\ell_{2}
-cover of
\Theta_{0}
at radius
\varepsilon/U
induces an
L_{1}(Q)
-cover of
\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}}
at radius
\varepsilon
.

Step 2: slicing

Now decompose
\Theta_{0}
according to supports. For each subset
S\subseteq\{1,\dots,d\}
with
|S|\leq s
, let

\Theta_{S}:=\{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\theta_{j}=0\forall j\not\in S,\ \|\theta\|_{2}\leq R\}.

Then

\Theta_{0}=\bigcup_{|S|\leq s}\Theta_{S}.

For fixed
S
,
\Theta_{S}
is an Euclidean ball of radius
R
in dimension
|S|\leq s
, so its
\ell_{2}
-covering number satisfies for any
Q
on
x_{1},\cdots,x_{N_{\mathcal{X}}}
,

\mathcal{N}\!\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{U},\Theta_{S},||\cdot||_{2}\right)\leq\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s}.

Moreover, the number of supports of size at most
s
is bounded by

\sum_{j=0}^{s}\binom{d}{j}\leq\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right)^{s}.

By Lemma
D.8
, and using the bound on the covering number under the
l_{1}
-norm that follows from Equation (
F.3
), this implies,

\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}},L_{1}(Q))\leq\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right)^{s}\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right)^{s}.

Step 3: Dudley’s entropy integral bound

Hence

\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}},L_{1}(Q))}\,d\varepsilon\leq\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{s\log\!\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right)+s\log\!\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right)}\,d\varepsilon.

Using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
, we can write

\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}},L_{1}(Q))}\,d\varepsilon\leq 2RU\sqrt{s\log\!\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right)}+\sqrt{s}\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\!\left(1+\frac{2RU}{\varepsilon}\right)}\,d\varepsilon.

With the change of variables
t=\varepsilon/(2RU)
, the second term becomes

2RU\sqrt{s}\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{\log(1+t^{-1})}\,dt\leq c_{1}RU\sqrt{s}

for a universal constant
c_{1}<\infty
. Therefore, for a universal constant
c_{2}<\infty
,

\sup_{Q\in\mathcal{Q}}\int_{0}^{2RU}\sqrt{\log\mathcal{N}(\varepsilon,\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}},L_{1}(Q))}\,d\varepsilon\leq c_{2}RU\sqrt{s\log\!\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right)}.

Thus Assumption
3.3
holds with

C(\mathcal{F}_{\ell_{0}})\leq c_{2}^{2}R^{2}U^{2}\,s\log\!\left(\frac{ed}{s}\right).

The result follows directly from Theorem
3.1
.

F.4
Proof of Proposition
A.4

We apply Theorem
3.4
with
\bar{c}
in place of
c
. This yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[R_{\bar{c}}(\hat{f}^{\,\bar{c}},\hat{\pi}^{\,\bar{c}})-\min_{f\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq c_{0}J^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{M_{0}+M_{0}^{2}}{|\mathcal{X}|}}+\inf_{1\leq j\leq J}\left\{c_{0}\sqrt{\tilde{M}_{u}\,\frac{C(\mathcal{F}_{j})+v_{\Pi_{j}}}{|\mathcal{X}|}}+\Delta_{j}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\pi_{j,\bar{c}}^{\star})+A_{\bar{c}}(\pi_{j,\bar{c}}^{\star})\right\}.

Since
\bar{c}(x)\geq c(x)
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

R_{c}(f,\pi)\leq R_{\bar{c}}(f,\pi)

for all
(f,\pi)
. On the other hand,
the no-abstention benchmark

\min_{f\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}

does not depend on the abstention cost. Therefore

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\!\left[R_{c}(\hat{f}^{\,\bar{c}},\hat{\pi}^{\,\bar{c}})-\min_{f\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\!\left[R_{\bar{c}}(\hat{f}^{\,\bar{c}},\hat{\pi}^{\,\bar{c}})-\min_{f\in\bar{\mathcal{F}}}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\bigl(f(x)-\tau(x)\bigr)^{2}\right],

which proves the claim.

F.5
Proof of Theorem
A.5

Following the argument of Equation (
B.1
) it suffices to bound
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right].

By Brook’s theorem (following a similar argument as in
Viviano (2024)
), we can partition units
x\in\mathcal{X}
into
L+1
disjoint groups
\{\mathcal{X}_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L+1},\cup_{l=1}^{L+1}\mathcal{X}_{l}=\mathcal{X}
, with independent observations within each group. Therefore, by the triangular inequality, we can write

\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}-R_{c})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right]\leq\sum_{l=1}^{L+1}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}^{l}-R_{c}^{l})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right].

where we denote
R_{c}^{L}=\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}_{l}}p(x)\left\{(f(x)-\tau(x))^{2}\pi(x)+c(x)(1-\pi(x))\right\}
and
\widehat{R}^{l}
the empirical analog. We follow then verbatim the proof of Theorem
3.1
to bound each component
\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{\pi\in\Pi,f\in\mathcal{F}}\bigl|(\widehat{R}^{l}-R_{c}^{l})(f,\pi)\bigr|\right]
.

F.6
Proof of Proposition
A.6

Denote by
\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\mathrm{un}}
the unrestricted depth-
L
tree class solved by Algorithm
1
, and by
\mathcal{P}_{L,G}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\mathrm{un}}
the constrained subclass with at most
G-1
predictive leaves. Let

E_{L}^{\star}:=\inf_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\mathrm{un}}}\widehat{R}(f,\pi)

be the empirical optimum returned by Algorithm
1
. Algorithm
A.2
returns a pruned rule
(\hat{f}^{t},\hat{\pi}^{t})
and reports

\varepsilon:=\widehat{R}(\hat{f}^{t},\hat{\pi}^{t})-E_{L}^{\star}.

Since
\mathcal{P}_{L,G}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{L}^{\mathrm{un}}
, we have

E_{L}^{\star}\leq\inf_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}\widehat{R}(f,\pi).

Therefore,

\widehat{R}(\hat{f}^{t},\hat{\pi}^{t})\leq\inf_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}\widehat{R}(f,\pi)+\varepsilon.

Thus
\varepsilon
is an observable upper bound on the empirical optimization error of the pruned rule relative to the constrained class
\mathcal{P}_{L,G}
.

Repeating the basic inequality used in the proof of Theorem
3.1
(Equation (
B.1
)),

R_{c}(\hat{f}^{t},\hat{\pi}^{t})-\inf_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}R_{c}\!\left(f,\pi\right)\leq 2\sup_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}\left|\widehat{R}\!\left(f,\pi\right)-R_{c}\!\left(f,\pi\right)\right|+\varepsilon.

Following verbatim Step 1-Step 4 in the proof of Theorem
3.1
, and using the complexity bound in the proof of Theorem
3.2
(Step 3), we can write

\mathbb{E}\left[2\sup_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}\left|\widehat{R}\!\left(f,\pi\right)-R_{c}\!\left(f,\pi\right)\right|\right]\leq q_{0}\bar{p}\max\{1,K^{2}\}B^{3/2}\sqrt{\tilde{M}_{u}\,\frac{(v+1)\log G}{\underline{\kappa}|\mathcal{X}|}},

where
v
is the largest VC complexity of
x\mapsto 1\{\alpha(x)=g\},g\in\{1,\cdots,G\}
.
Using Markov’s inequality, it follows that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

2\sup_{(f,\pi)\in\mathcal{P}_{L,G}}\left|\widehat{R}\!\left(f,\pi\right)-R_{c}\!\left(f,\pi\right)\right|\leq\frac{q_{0}}{\delta}\bar{p}\max\{1,K^{2}\}B^{3/2}\sqrt{\tilde{M}_{u}\,\frac{(v+1)\log G}{\underline{\kappa}|\mathcal{X}|}}.

Since
v\leq dG\log(G)
by Example
3.1
,
the proof completes after collecting the terms.

F.7
Proof of Theorem
E.1

We will follow similarly the proof of Theorem
4.2
with changes in notation whenever necessary (and with a slight abuse of notation).

Notation

Before deriving the theorem, we introduce some notation. Let

\displaystyle\xi_{x}^{(m)}=\Big\{(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}-(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}\Big\}\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x)

(F.4)

and similarly denote

\sigma_{m}^{2}:=|\mathcal{X}|\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)^{2}\mathbb{V}\!\left(\xi_{x}^{(m)}\mid\mathcal{T}\right),\qquad\mathcal{T}:=\left((\hat{f}^{(m^{\prime})},\hat{\pi}^{(m^{\prime})})_{m^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}},\hat{f}^{(0)}\right)

(F.5)

Note that because
p(x)^{2}
is of order
1/N_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}
, we expect
\sigma_{m}^{2}(f)=O(1)
.
We write

\displaystyle\widehat{\Delta}(m)=\sum_{x}p(x)\xi_{x}^{(m)},\hskip 18.49988pt\Delta(m):=\sum_{x}p(x)\Big\{(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}-(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}\Big\}\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x).

(F.6)

Note that because
\mathbb{E}[(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}|\mathcal{T}]=\eta(x)^{2}+(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}
and similarly
\mathbb{E}[(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}|\mathcal{T}]=\eta(x)^{2}+(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\tau(x))^{2}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Delta}(m)|\mathcal{T}]=\Delta(m)
.
To avoid degenerate solutions (i.e., the variance equals zero), we assume that the relevant variance is bounded away from zero.

To establish asymptotic normality, we first derive the following lemma.

Lemma F.1

(Marginal asymptotic normality of the break-even statistic)
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.3
hold for the out of sample estimates, and assume the out-of-sample estimates are independent of
\mathcal{T}
(in-sample estimates).
Take any
m\in\mathcal{M}
which is
\mathcal{T}
-measurable. Let
\sigma_{m}^{2}>\underline{l}
for a constant
\underline{l}>0
almost surely, with
\sigma_{m}^{2}
as defined in Equation (
F.5
). Then

\frac{\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}|}\Big(\widehat{\Delta}(m)-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Delta}(m)|\mathcal{T}]\Big)}{\sigma_{m}}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}N(0,1).

Proof of Lemma
F.1
.

We will write
\xi_{x}:=\xi_{x}^{(m)}
whenever clear from the context.
By Assumption
3.1
(independence), and independence of the training and out-of-sample estimates, the random variables

p(x)\Big(\xi_{x}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{x}\mid\mathcal{T}]\Big),

are conditionally independent and centered given
\mathcal{T}
.

Therefore, we establish the central limit theorem using Lyapunov central limit theorem conditional on
\mathcal{T}
.
We first verify a uniform third-moment bound. Write

A_{x}^{(m)}:=\Big\{(\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}\Big\}\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x),\qquad A_{x}^{(0)}:=(\hat{f}^{(0)}(x)-\hat{\tau}_{oos}(x))^{2}\hat{\pi}^{(m)}(x),

so that
\xi_{x}=A_{x}^{(m)}-A_{x}^{(0)}
. By Lemma
D.7
, Assumption
3.1
(ii) (the uniform bound
|\tau(x)|\leq B
) and the envelope bound
|\hat{f}^{(m)}(x)|,|\hat{f}^{(0)}|\leq K
in Assumption
3.3
, there exists a finite constant
c_{0}<\infty
, that only depends on
M_{0},B,K<\infty
, so that

\max\Big\{\mathbb{E}\big[|A_{x}^{(m)}-\mathbb{E}[A_{x}^{(m)}\mid\mathcal{T}]|^{3}\mid\mathcal{T}\big],\mathbb{E}\big[|A_{x}^{(0)}-\mathbb{E}[A_{x}^{(0)}\mid\mathcal{T}]|^{3}\mid\mathcal{T}\big]\Big\}\leq c_{0}<\infty.

Hence, since
|a-b|^{3}\leq 4(|a|^{3}+|b|^{3})
, for a finite constant
c_{1}<\infty
(a function of
c_{0}
)

\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|\xi_{x}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{x}\mid\mathcal{T}]\big|^{3}\mid\mathcal{T}\Big]\leq c_{1}<\infty.

By Assumption
3.1
(iii),

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)^{3}\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)^{2}\leq\frac{\bar{p}}{|\mathcal{X}|}\cdot\frac{\bar{p}}{|\mathcal{X}|}=\frac{\bar{p}^{2}}{|\mathcal{X}|^{2}}.

Therefore

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|p(x)\big(\xi_{x}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{x}\mid\mathcal{T}]\big)\big|^{3}\mid\mathcal{T}\Big]\leq c_{1}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}p(x)^{3}\leq c_{1}\bar{p}^{2}|\mathcal{X}|^{-2}.

Since
\sigma_{m}^{2}>\underline{l}
for a positive constant
\underline{l}>0
by assumption, it follows that

\frac{|\mathcal{X}|^{3/2}\sum_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\big|p(x)\big(\xi_{x}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{x}\mid\mathcal{T}]\big)\big|^{3}\mid\mathcal{T}\Big]}{\sigma_{m}^{3}}\longrightarrow 0.

Thus the Lyapunov condition holds conditionally on
\mathcal{T}
. Therefore, it follows that as we define
Z_{|\mathcal{X}|}=\frac{\sqrt{|\mathcal{X}|}\Big(\widehat{\Delta}(m)-\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Delta}(m)|\mathcal{T}]\Big)}{\sigma_{m}}
, we have
P(Z_{|\mathcal{X}|}\leq t|\mathcal{T})\rightarrow\Phi(t)
where
\Phi(t)
is the Gaussian CDF. Integrating over
\mathcal{T}
over both sides and applying the dominated convergence theorem gives the
unconditional convergence stated in the lemma.
∎

The rest of the proof mimics the proof of Theorem
4.2
and omitted for brevity.
